Let me analyze the input file:

1. It includes `sll_nodata_def.h` - so the reference is `references/sll_nodata.md` and the lib is `sll_nodata_shape_lib`
2. The function `multi_append` takes three `struct list *` parameters: x, y, z
3. The loop traverses list `x` via `u = t->next`, and in the body, it interleaves nodes from `y` into the list

Let me understand the loop:
- Before the loop: `t = x`, `u = t->next` (so `u = x->next`)
- The while loop condition is `while (u)` - iterates as long as `u` is not null
- Inside, if `y` is non-null, it inserts a node from `y` between `t` and `u`:
  - `t->next = y` (point t's next to y)
  - `t = y` (advance t to y)
  - `y = y->next` (advance y to its next)
  - `t->next = u` (point new t's next to u) - wait, we set t to y, so y->next = u, this inserts y between original t and u
  - `t = u` (advance t to u)
  - `u = u->next` (advance u)
- If `y` is null: append u and z, set t->next to result, return x

The loop takes elements from the `y` list and splices them into the list starting at `x`, between each pair of original `x` nodes. So it's interleaving nodes from `y` into the list.

After the loop (when `u` becomes null, i.e., we've reached the end of the original `x` list):
- `t->next = append(y, z)` - append the rest of `y` and all of `z` to the end
- return `x`

First, let me understand what's happening in the loop. The function `multi_append`:

1. If `x == 0`, it just appends y and z and returns.
2. Otherwise: `t = x`, `u = t->next` (i.e., `u = x->next`)
3. The while loop runs while `u != 0`:
   - If `y != 0`: Takes one node from `y`, inserts it between the current `t` and `u` nodes, then advances.
   - If `y == 0`: Appends `u` and `z`, returns.
4. After the loop (u becomes 0, meaning end of original x list): `t->next = append(y, z)`, return `x`.

So the invariant: The loop is iterating through the original `x` list (tracked by `t` and `u`), while taking nodes from `y` and inserting them one at a time.

```

    t -> next = y;  // t's next now points to y's first node
    t = y;          // t advances to y
    y = y -> next;  // y advances to y->next

```

- Advances t to u and u to u->next

Wait, that's interesting! x_k's next field was never modified. Only x_{k-1}->next was modified (in step 1 of iteration k). And y_k->next was modified (in step 4). But original x nodes' next fields are only modified when they are "t" (i.e., when t = x_i, step 1 changes x_i->next to y_{i+1}).

So at the loop head: t = x_k, and x_k's next field has NOT been modified yet (it still points to x_{k+1} = u). SO: `t->next == u`.

That's an important invariant! `u == t->next`.

Wait, but this only holds at the loop condition. Let me verify:

At the loop condition after iteration 0 (before first iteration):
- t = x_0 = x
- u = x_0->next = x_1
- t->next = x_0->next = x_1 = u ✓

At the loop condition after iteration 1:
- t = x_1 (which is y_307 in the RPs)
- u = x_2 (which is y_320)
- x_1->next was originally x_2, and it was never modified (only x_0->next was modified)
- So t->next = x_1->next = x_2 = u ✓

At the loop condition after iteration 2:
- t = x_2 (which is y_320 in RP_iter_3)
- u = x_3 (which is y_336)
- x_2->next was not modified in iteration 2 (only x_1->next was modified, setting it to y_2)
- So t->next = x_2->next = x_3 = u ✓

Great! So `u == t->next` is a stable invariant.

But wait - this changes when the loop is entered. In iteration (k+1), step 1 modifies t->next (where t = x_k), setting it to y_{k+1}. Then by step 5, t = x_{k+1}, and the invariant is restored: u (= x_{k+2}) == t->next (= x_{k+1}->next).

So `u == t->next` holds at every loop condition check. Very useful!

Also, let me check: does this property hold in the RP data?

- `data_at(&(x@pre->next), struct list*, y_307)` — x->next = y_307

- `data_at(u_300_addr, struct list*, y_307)` — u = y_307

So `u == t->next` is validated in all RPs. 

Actually wait, for the invariant we don't use the existentially quantified names. We use program variable names. So the PROP would be: `u == t->next`.

In RP_pre_loop, the PROP only says `x@pre != 0`.

Common to all RPs:
- `x != 0` — yes, present in all three

Let me think about `u != 0`. The invariant is examined at the loop condition. When u == 0, we exit and the invariant still needs to hold (but QCP treats this differently - the invariant is verified at the loop head for all paths, and the loop exit condition is handled by the loop rule). But in our case, the RPs are only recorded for iterations where u != 0 (since we enter the loop). So u != 0 is in all the workable RPs.

But for the invariant to be valid we need it to hold even when u == 0 (i.e., we need the invariant to allow u == 0). Does the invariant `lseg(x, 0) * listrep(0) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)` make sense? lseg(x, 0) is the whole list (a listrep). listrep(0) is emp. So the overall expression would be listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z). This makes perfect sense!

- The loop is about to exit
- lseg(x, 0) = listrep(x) (the full merged list from x)

- listrep(y) * listrep(z) — the remaining y and z lists

The postcondition of multi_append requires listrep(__return). If we return x, and x points to the merged list with the rest of y and z appended... That's handled by the code after the loop, not by the invariant.

```

This appends the remaining y list and the full z list to the end of the merged list. But the postcondition is just `listrep(__return)` where __return = x (the return value). Since x points to the merged list (which is listrep), this holds.

listrep(0) = emp
listrep(y) — remaining y
listrep(z) — z

`u = t->next` is set before the loop. And it holds at every loop condition. Adding `u == t->next` helps the verifier connect u and t when unfolding/refolding predicates.

Wait, actually I want to double-check: is `has_permission(&t)` about the variable t or about the pointer value? It's about the variable — giving permission to access the memory pointed to by t. Since t is a pointer variable in the C code, `has_permission(&t)` gives permission to dereference t (i.e., read/write `t->next`).

Hmm, but in QCP, t is a local variable stored at address &t. `has_permission(&t)` I think means permission to access the local variable t's storage. That doesn't give permission to dereference t as a pointer.

So `has_permission(&t)` gives the right to dereference t. OK good.

lseg(x, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(0) * listrep(z) = lseg(x, u) * listrep(u) * emp * listrep(z)
= lseg(x, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(z)

The `grep -c` returns 0 when there are 0 matches. In that case, the exit code is 1 (grep returns 1 when no match). So `|| echo "0 (file not found)"` would be triggered! Wait, `grep -c "Admitted\." file 2>/dev/null`:
- If file exists and "Admitted." appears N times -> outputs N, exit 0
- If file exists and "Admitted." appears 0 times -> outputs 0, exit 1
- If file doesn't exist -> error to /dev/null, exit 2

So with ||:
- Case 0 matches: outputs "0", exit 1 → fallback runs → outputs "0 (file not found)"
- Case N matches: outputs "N", exit 0 → no fallback
- File not found: error to /dev/null, exit 2 → fallback runs → outputs "0 (file not found)"

So the output "0\n0 (file not found)" means: 0 matches (no Admitted), but the || fallback also ran. This is because grep exits 1 when 0 matches. So the proof_manual.v exists, has 0 Admitted.

2. See that the last node of lseg(x, u) has next = u (from lseg definition)
3. Conclude that t = the last node of lseg(x, u)
4. Thus, t is inside lseg(x, u) and has permission

- listrep(y_first → y_rest) = data_at(y_first→next, y_rest) * listrep(y_rest)

- The y-node was spliced: t_old→y_first→u_old
- Then t = u_old, u = u_old->next

New state:
- lseg(x, u_old) = lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u_old)
- data_at(u_old->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) = listrep(u_old)

So the re-established invariant:
lseg(x, u_old) * data_at(u_old->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * listrep(y_rest)
= lseg(x, t') * data_at(t'->next, u') * listrep(u') * listrep(y')

This is correct in theory. But the strategies need to:
1. Unfold listrep(y_old) to extract y_first
2. Unfold listrep(u_old) to extract u_old->next
3. Modify t_old->next to y_first
4. Set y_first->next = u_old
5. Set u_old->next (already set) — actually, step 6 reads u_old->next but doesn't modify it
6. Fold lseg(x, u_old) from lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u_old)
7. Re-establish data_at(u_old->next, u_new) and listrep(u_new)

The folding of lseg is the tricky part. The strategies need a rule: "if you have lseg(x, t) * data_at(x->next, y) * data_at(y->next, t') then you can fold to lseg(x, t')" but this is under the assumption that the chain goes x → ... → t → y → t'.

Actually, lseg(x, t') definition:
lseg(x, t') = (x == t' && emp) || data_at(x->next, z) * lseg(z, t')

If we unfold lseg(x, t') until z = something we know:
We have lseg(x, t) which is from x to t. And then data_at(t->next, y). And data_at(y->next, t').

But lseg(x, t') would be: unfolding from x: data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, t')
Eventually: ... * data_at(..., t) * lseg(t, t')
lseg(t, t') = (t == t' && emp) || data_at(t->next, y) * lseg(y, t')
And lseg(y, t') = (y == t' && emp) || data_at(y->next, t') * lseg(t', t') = data_at(y->next, t')

So lseg(x, t') = lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, t')

Which is exactly what we have! So folding is correct in theory.

I think the issue is that the STRATEGIES don't have this folding rule. They might only have rules for unfolding lseg, not folding it.

But wait, the strategies need to check the entailment, not fold the predicate. In QCP, the entailment `A |-- B` means "A entails B". If the post-body state entails the invariant, it should pass. The post-body state has:
data_at chains that correspond to the full unfolded lseg(x, t'), and the strategies should be able to CHECK that lseg(x, t') follows from these data_ats.

But the way QCP handles this might be: it unfolds lseg(x, t') in the INVARIANT (right side), and tries to match the unfolded predicates against the post-body state (left side). So it would:
1. Unfold lseg(x, t') → data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, t')
2. Match data_at(x->next, z1) against the post-body state
3. Continue unfolding

But this would be a recursive check that the strategies need to handle. The strategies might limit how deep they unfold, or they might need specific rules.

- lseg(x, y) = (x == y && emp) || exists z, data_at(field_addr(x, next), ..., z) * lseg(z, y)

lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, u_old) * listrep(u_old) * listrep(y_old) * listrep(z)

During body:
1. unfold listrep(y_old) to get first node y_first: listrep(y_old) = data_at(y_first->next, y_rest) * listrep(y_rest)
2. write t_old->next = y_first: data_at(t_old->next, y_first) replaces data_at(t_old->next, u_old)
3. write y_first->next = u_old: data_at(y_first->next, u_old) replaces data_at(y_first->next, y_rest)
4. unfold listrep(u_old) to advance u: listrep(u_old) = data_at(u_old->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new)
5. t = u_old, u = u_new, y = y_rest

Post-body state:
lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u_old) * data_at(u_old->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

Now we need to show this |-- lseg(x, u_old) * data_at(u_old->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

The question: does lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u_old) ENT ail lseg(x, u_old)?

From lseg definition:
lseg(x, u_old) unfolds to: 
lseg(x, u_old) = data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, u_old)

Since lseg(x, t_old) already exists, we can think of it as:
lseg(x, t_old) unfolds to data_ats ending at the node whose next = t_old.

Then lseg(t_old, u_old) = (t_old == u_old && emp) || data_at(t_old->next, z) * lseg(z, u_old)

Since t_old != u_old in general:
lseg(t_old, u_old) = data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * lseg(y_first, u_old)
lseg(y_first, u_old) = data_at(y_first->next, u_old) * lseg(u_old, u_old) = data_at(y_first->next, u_old)

So lseg(t_old, u_old) = data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u_old)

And lseg(x, u_old) = lseg(x, t_old) * lseg(t_old, u_old) = lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u_old) ✓

So the entailment should hold, but it requires the strategies to know that lseg can be reassembled like this.

I think the strategies simply don't support this folding pattern. The strategies might only handle:
- listrep → data_at * listrep (unfold at head)
- data_at * listrep → listrep (fold at head)
- lseg → data_at * lseg (unfold at head)

They don't have rules for "if you have A * B * C and A * B = lseg(x, y) when combined with the right C, then you can fold."

This is a limitation of the automatic strategies. For complex invariants, we might need to adjust the Coq proofs manually.

But SKILL.md says to retry with different invariants. Let me think about what other invariants might work.

Let me re-examine the problem. The loop in multi_append:
1. Traverses the original x list using t/u
2. Splices nodes from y between the nodes of x
3. The else branch exits early

The key challenge is that the strategies can't handle the complex reshaping of the list.

Maybe a better invariant uses the "processed prefix" approach with `lseg(x, u)` but without needing to find t inside it. Let me think...

What if we use a PROP constraint that ties t to the lseg? Like `node_in_lseg(t, x, u)`? But there's no such predicate in sll_nodata.

Or what if we add `lseg(t, u)` as a separate predicate? That would make t a boundary explicitly:
- `lseg(x, t) * listrep(t) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)`

Where `lseg(x, t)` is the processed prefix (up to but not including t) and `listrep(t)` is the remaining x list starting at t (which includes u and beyond).

At the loop head:
- After 0 iterations: t = x, lseg(x, x) = emp, listrep(x) covers everything
- After 1 iteration: t = x1, lseg(x, x1) covers x → y1, listrep(x1) covers x1 → x2 → ...

For the body:
- To access t->next, we unfold listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u)
- To access u->next, we unfold listrep(u) = data_at(u->next, u2) * listrep(u2)
- To access y->next, we unfold listrep(y) = data_at(y->next, y2) * listrep(y2)

After the body, the new t is u, and the new u is u->next:
- lseg(x, u) = lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u) [where y_first was spliced in]
- listrep(u) = data_at(u->next, u2) * listrep(u2) [already unrolled]

Wait, but the strategies need to fold the modified lseg:
After modification: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u) = lseg(x, u)

This folding requires the strategies to extend lseg(x, t) with two new data_ats. They might not have this rule.

But what about `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u)` — can the strategies match this against:
lseg(x, u) = data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, u)
           // unfolding lseg(x, t) gives data_ats ending at t
           = data_at(x->next, z1) * data_at(z1->next, z2) * ... * data_at(zn->next, t) * lseg(t, u)
where lseg(t, u) = data_at(t->next, y) * lseg(y, u)
                 = data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u) * lseg(u, u)
                 = data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u)

So: lseg(x, u) = [already existing lseg(x, t)] * data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u)

For the strategies to prove this, they'd need to unfold lseg(x, u) from the INVARIANT's lseg(x, u) (right side) and match the data_ats one by one. They need deep unfolding support.

Actually wait, in the `lseg(x, t) * data_at(&(t->next), ..., u)` approach, the invariant SEP is:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

After the body, we need to show:
lseg(x, u) * data_at(u->next, u2) * listrep(u2) * listrep(y2) * listrep(z)

For the return path, we need to show from the invariant with u = 0:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, 0) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)
After t->next = append(y, z):
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result) |-- listrep(x)

For this, the strategies need to show that lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result) can be folded into listrep(x). This is:
listrep(x) = lseg(x, t) * listrep(t)
listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result)

So listrep(x) = lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result) ✓

This is: listrep(x) = lseg(x, t) * listrep(t) = lseg(x, t) * (data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result))

Where we need `listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result)`. Since t is the NODE, and t->next = result, listrep(t) starting at t = data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result). This is correct.

But again, the strategies need a rule for this.

Let me check if sll_nodata has this rule by looking at the reference. The reference says:

```
listrep(l) = (l == 0 && emp) || exists t, data_at(field_addr(l, next), struct list*, t) * listrep(t)
```

This defines listrep. To CHECK the entailment, the strategies need to:
1. Take the RHS: listrep(x)
2. UNFOLD it: x != 0 → data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z)
3. Match this against the LHS: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result)

But the LHS doesn't have data_at(x->next, z). It has lseg(x, t) which might begin with data_at(x->next, ...). If x == t, then lseg(x, t) = emp, and the LHS is:
data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result) = data_at(x->next, result) * listrep(result)

And the RHS unfolded is data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z). Matching: z = result ✓.

But if x != t (i.e., after the first iteration), the LHS is:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result)

lseg(x, t) starts with data_at(x->next, ...), so the LHS starts with data_at(x->next, some_value). And the RHS starts with data_at(x->next, z). These should match! Then the strategies continue with the rest of lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result) vs listrep(z).

But wait, RHS says listrep(x) → data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z). The first data_at matches. Then we need to match the rest of LHS against listrep(z).

The rest of LHS is: lseg(z, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result) [since lseg(x, t) = data_at(x->next, z) * lseg(z, t)]

And RHS continued is: listrep(z)

We'd need listrep(z) = lseg(z, t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result).

Continuing: listrep(z) → data_at(z->next, z2) * listrep(z2). LHS has: data_at(z->next, ...) * lseg(..., t) * data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result). If z is the node before t, then data_at(z->next, t) * lseg(t, t) = data_at(z->next, t) * emp. 

So lseg(z, t) = data_at(z->next, t) (if z != t) or emp (if z = t).

Then data_at(z->next, t) matches against data_at(z->next, z2) from listrep(z), so z2 = t.

Then listrep(z2) = listrep(t) should match the remaining LHS: data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result).

listrep(t) → data_at(t->next, t2) * listrep(t2). And LHS has: data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result). Matching: t2 = result, listrep(t2) = listrep(result) ✓.

So the full matching does work. The strategies need to:
1. Unfold listrep(x) repeatedly (each time matching a data_at from the LHS side)
2. For the return_wit, unfold until we reach t
3. Then match data_at(t->next, result) * listrep(result) against listrep(t)

This requires the strategies to support arbitrary-depth unfolding of listrep/lseg to match the predicate structure. If they only support 1-level unfolding, they can't verify the return path when there are multiple nodes between x and t.

The unroll depth is 3, so the strategies should unfold up to 3 levels. This should be enough for lists up to 3 nodes long.

But maybe the strategies' unfolding is limited in a different way. They might only unfold at specific positions, not as a general matching algorithm.

Now I can see the actual goals:

**entail_wit_2**: The LHS (after executing the if-body) needs to entail the RHS (invariant re-establishment):

LHS (post-body state):
- PROP: x != 0, u != 0, y_3 != 0
- data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) [listrep(u) unfolded]
- data_at(y_3->next, u) [y_3 is the first node of y's list, y_3->next written to u]
- listrep(y) [rest of y]
- lseg(x, t) 
- data_at(t->next, y_3) [t->next changed to y_3]
- listrep(z)

RHS (invariant):
- PROP: x != 0
- lseg(x, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

So the key entailment is:
`lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u) * listrep(u) |-- lseg(x, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2)`

Wait, I already read listrep(u) on the LHS as unfolded to data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2). So the LHS has:
... * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * ...

And the RHS has:
... * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * ...

These are the same! So the only difference is `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)` vs `lseg(x, u)`.

So the entailment is: `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u) |-- lseg(x, u)`

This is exactly what I analyzed before. The strategies need to fold lseg(x, t) + 2 data_ats into lseg(x, u).

**return_wit_1**: Normal loop exit (u = 0), after `t->next = append(y, z)`:

LHS: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) (where retval is the append result)
RHS: listrep(x)

Entailment: `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(x)`

This is: lseg(x, t) * listrep(t) = listrep(x), where listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, v) * listrep(v) for any v.

**return_wit_2**: Early return (y = 0), after `u = append(u, z); t->next = u; return x;`

LHS: same as return_wit_1: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)
RHS: listrep(x)

Wait, but in retrun_wit_2 there's also u != 0, y = 0. The `listrep retval` is the result of append(u, z).

So the same entailment as return_wit_1:
`lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(x)`

Both return_wit goals are the SAME identity:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) = lseg(x, t) * listrep(t) = listrep(x)

And the entail_wit_2 is also the same concept:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u) = lseg(x, u)

All three failures are about the SAME thing: the strategies can't fold lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, ...) * [more data_ats] into lseg(x, [next_node]) or listrep(x).

So the root cause is clear: the sll_nodata strategies don't support EXTENDING an lseg with new nodes. They only support unfolding lseg at the head.

This is a fundamental limitation of the automatic strategies. The question is: can we work around it with a different invariant that doesn't require lseg folding?

Let me think about what invariant would avoid this issue...

The key constraint is that we need to describe the state at the loop head in a way that:
1. Is established at entry (before the loop)
2. Is preserved by the loop body
3. Can be used to prove the loop exit postcondition
4. Doesn't require the strategies to do complex folding

What if the invariant only uses `listrep` (no `lseg` and no `data_at`)? 

`listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)`

But this fails with "Cannot derive the precondition of Memory Read" because QCP can't find t inside listrep(x) to grant permission for t->next access.

What if we add `has_permission(&t)` to solve the permission issue? But `has_permission(&t)` causes dup_data_at with the stack frame.

Wait, let me reconsider. In the `listrep` invariant, the "Cannot derive precondition" error happened on `u = u->next` (line 30), not on `t->next = y` (line 27). Let me re-examine.

Actually, in the `listrep` run, the error was:
```
fatal error: Cannot derive the precondition of Memory Read. in ... multi_append_inv.c:30:14
```

Line 30 is `u = u -> next;`. So QCP can't prove that reading `u->next` is safe.

With `listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)`:
- u = t->next, and t is inside listrep(x)
- So u is inside listrep(x) and `u->next` should be accessible by unfolding listrep(x)

But QCP doesn't know u's position in listrep(x). The strategies would need to unfold listrep(x) to find u, which they can't do.

The `lseg(x, t) * data_at(&(t->next), ..., u) * listrep(u)` approach solves the PERMISSION issue because:
- data_at(t->next, u) explicitly gives permission to access t->next
- listrep(u) explicitly gives permission to access u->next

So the body operations are safe with this invariant. The only issue is the entailment after the body.

Now, for the entailment issue, the problem is folding lseg + data_ats. What if I use `lseg` everywhere instead?

Something like: `lseg(x, u) * lseg(u, 0) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)`

Where `lseg(u, 0)` covers from u to the end (0), and `listrep(y)` and `listrep(z)` are the remaining lists.

But `lseg(u, 0) = listrep(u)` in terms of coverage. And we still need to find u inside this to modify it.

Hmm, what about using a different boundary? Instead of `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u)`, what about:

`listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * has_permission(&u) * has_permission(&t)`

The dup_data_at for `has_permission` was specifically for `t_297_addr` — the STACK address of local variable t. What if has_permission for u is OK?

Actually wait, let me re-check. The `has_permission(&t)` might create a data_at for `t_297_addr`. But the stack frame also has `data_at(t_297_addr, ..., t_value)`. Do these conflict?

The dup_data_at error was for `t_297_addr` — the same address was claimed by two data_ats:
1. The stack frame's data_at for t
2. has_permission(&t)'s data_at for t

But what about `has_permission(&u)`? The stack frame has `data_at(u_300_addr, ..., u_value)`. And `has_permission(&u)` would create a data_at for `u_300_addr`. This would also conflict!

So `has_permission` is fundamentally incompatible with QCP's stack variable tracking in this version.

What if I use a different mechanism to grant permission? 

Looking at the QCP syntax, maybe there's an alternative. What about using `data_at` for the variable's address directly, but without the variable name?

Actually, let me reconsider. In the `lseg(x, t) * data_at(&(t->next), ..., u)` invariant, permissions work fine — the operations in the loop body are all safe. The only issue is the ENTAILMENT at the end.

But that's a SEP equality, not a PROP constraint. PROP constraints are C expressions.

What if instead of lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u), we use lseg(x, u) with a different mechanism?

OK wait, I just had an important insight. Looking at the `partial_solve_wit` that ARE handled automatically:

lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)
→ EX y_2, data_at(y->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y) * listrep(u) * listrep(z)

This is: unfold listrep(y) at the head to get data_at(y->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2). This is the standard unfold rule. The strategies handle this.

`partial_solve_wit_4`:

Again, standard unfold.

1. EXTEND lseg(x, t) to lseg(x, u) with the data_ats in between

For issue 1, the strategies might need a specific rule about combining lseg with data_at.

For issue 2, the strategies need a rule: `lseg(a, b) * data_at(b->next, c) * listrep(c) |-- listrep(a)`.

LHS from code: listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z), with t = x, u = x->next
RHS invariant: lseg(x, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

Where lseg(x, u) = data_at(x->next, u) * lseg(u, u) * emp = data_at(x->next, u)
And listrep(x) = data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u)

So LHS = data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)
RHS = data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

These should be the same! The strategies should be able to unfold listrep(x) one step and match.

But they can't! Which means the strategies don't know that `listrep(x) = data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u)` where u = x->next. This should be the standard unfold, but maybe the strategies need u to be explicitly connected to the existential.

Wait, looking at the condition: u = t->next, and before the loop, t = x, so u = x->next. The strategies should unfold listrep(x) to data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z) and then match z with u (since u = x->next).

But they might not have the match rule for `z == u` because u is a program variable and z is an existential. In the abstract state, `u` and `z` are different symbolic values. The strategies might not be able to equate them.

This is the fundamental limitation: the strategies can't match program variables with existential variables from predicate unfoldings.

So the `data_at(t->next, u)` approach is better because it explicitly exposes t and u without existential quantification.

OK, let me think about what invariant form might work with the strategies' capabilities.

The strategies CAN:
1. Unfold listrep(x) at the head: listrep(x) → data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z) [existential z]
2. Unfold lseg(x, t) at the head: lseg(x, t) → data_at(x->next, z) * lseg(z, t) [existential z]
3. Match data_at and listrep/lseg fragments
4. Handle basic pointer operations

The strategies CAN'T:
1. Match program variables with existential variables from unfolding
2. Fold multiple data_ats into an lseg
3. Deep-search inside a predicate

Given these capabilities, the invariant should:
- Use `listrep` or `lseg` that directly exposes the variables being accessed
- Avoid requiring folding of multiple data_ats

The `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u)` approach almost works (0 errors, 3 Admitted). The issue is the folding at the very end (return_wit) and after the body (entail_wit_2).

What if I change the return path? The return path needs `listrep(x)`. If the invariant directly provides enough information for the strategies to derive `listrep(x)` from the post-loop state...

Actually, looking at the return_wit goals:
```
LHS: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)
RHS: listrep(x)
```

After `t->next = append(y, z)`, the state is:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(x)

But BEFORE `t->next = append(y, z)`, at the loop exit (u = 0 with our invariant):
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, 0) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

Wait, at the loop exit, u = 0. Our invariant has `listrep(u) = listrep(0) = emp`. And we have `data_at(t->next, u) = data_at(t->next, 0)`. So the state at loop exit is:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, 0) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

After `t->next = append(y, z)`:
- listrep(y) and listrep(z) are consumed by append
- t->next is set to the result

So the state is: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)

And we need to prove listrep(x). Let me check if this is actually provable:

lseg(x, t) = the chain from x to the node before t (not including t)
data_at(t->next, retval) = t's next is retval
listrep(retval) = the list starting at retval

The full chain from x is: [lseg(x, t)] [t] → retval → [listrep(retval)]

Where lseg(x, t) ends with some_node->next = t.

So the full list from x is: x → ... → some_node → t → retval → [listrep(retval)]

And listrep(x) should describe exactly this.

In lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval), lseg(x, t) does NOT include t. So the full list is: lseg(x, t) (covers up to the node before t) + [the node t itself is in data_at(t->next, retval)] + [the rest].

But where is the ownership of node t itself? It's NOT in lseg(x, t) (lseg excludes t). It's NOT in data_at(t->next, retval) (that only covers t's NEXT field, not t itself). In sll_nodata, struct list only has one field (next). So "t itself" IS just the next field. So data_at(t->next, retval) DOES cover all of t's owned data.

Wait, but listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, z) * listrep(z). And the OWNER of t is whoever has listrep(t). In our invariant, we DON'T have listrep(t). We have:
- lseg(x, t) = covers up to (but not including) t
- data_at(t->next, retval) = covers t's one field
- listrep(retval) = covers from retval onwards

So the entire structure from x onwards is owned: [lseg(x, t)] + data_at(t->next, retval) + listrep(retval)

And this should be convertible to listrep(x). Let me prove:

listrep(x) unfolds from x:
listrep(x) = (x == 0 && emp) || data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z)

Since x != 0 (from PROP):
listrep(x) = data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z)

We need to show that lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) = data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z) for some z.

Let's unfold lseg(x, t):
lseg(x, t) = data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, t)

If z1 = t (i.e., x->next = t, which means x == t):
lseg(x, t) = emp (since x == t)
Then LHS = data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) = data_at(x->next, retval) * listrep(retval)
And RHS = data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z), so z = retval. ✓

If z1 != t:
Continue unfolding lseg(z1, t) = data_at(z1->next, z2) * lseg(z2, t)
Eventually: lseg(x, t) = data_at(x->next, z1) * data_at(z1->next, z2) * ... * data_at(zn->next, t) * lseg(t, t)
                       = data_at(x->next, z1) * data_at(z1->next, z2) * ... * data_at(zn->next, t) * emp

So LHS = [data_at(x->next, z1) * ... * data_at(zn->next, t)] * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)
      = data_at(x->next, z1) * [data_at(z1->next, z2) * ... * data_at(zn->next, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)]

Now, the second part: data_at(z1->next, z2) * ... * data_at(zn->next, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)

This is: data_at(z1->next, z2) * listrep(z2)  [by folding: the rest is listrep(z2)]

Wait, that's not right. Let me think again.

Actually, listrep(zn+1→next) for the chain from zn+1 onwards is:
listrep(z1) = data_at(z1->next, z2) * listrep(z2) 
            = data_at(z1->next, z2) * data_at(z2->next, z3) * listrep(z3)
            = ...
            = data_at(z1->next, z2) * ... * data_at(zn->next, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)

But we DON'T have listrep(z1). We have individual data_ats from lseg(x, t) + the final data_at.

For the folding: we need to fold from the END backwards:
listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)
listrep(zn) = data_at(zn->next, t) * listrep(t)
listrep(z_{n-1}) = data_at(z_{n-1}->next, zn) * listrep(zn)
... and so on, until
listrep(z1) = ...
listrep(x) = data_at(x->next, z1) * listrep(z1)

This requires folding from the inside out, which is not how the strategies typically work.
The strategies unfold from the OUTSIDE in (at the head).

So the strategies would need to:
1. Unfold RHS (listrep(x)) at the head: data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z)
2. Match data_at(x->next, z) with the first data_at from lseg(x, t): z = z1
3. Now need: lseg(z1, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(z1) (new entailment)
4. Unfold RHS (listrep(z1)): data_at(z1->next, z2) * listrep(z2)
5. Match with first data_at from lseg(z1, t): z2 = next existential
6. Continue...
7. Eventually: lseg(t, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(t)
8. lseg(t, t) = emp, so: data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(t)
9. This is: listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval), which should be valid BY DEFINITION.

So the entailment is valid but requires N+1 unfold steps (where N is the length of lseg(x, t)). Each step unfolds the head of listrep and matches a data_at from lseg.

STEP 9 is the key: the strategies need to know:
data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(t)

This is the FOLDING rule: listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval).

This should be a basic strategy rule! It's the definition of listrep:
listrep(l) = (l == 0 && emp) || exists t, data_at(field_addr(l, next), struct list*, t) * listrep(t)

So: listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) [when t != 0]

The strategies should unfold listrep(t) to get the RHS:
listrep(t) → data_at(t->next, z) * listrep(z)
and match z = retval.

But the issue is: the goal has `data_at(t->next, retval)` on the LHS and listrep(t) on the RHS. The strategies need to UNFOLD the RHS listrep(t) and match data_at(t->next, z) with LHS's data_at(t->next, retval). This requires matching the existential z with retval.

Can the strategies do this? They might not be able to match z with retval because retval is a free variable (a universally quantified variable in the goal) and z is a fresh existential.

Actually, in the entailment `A |-- B`, the RHS is the "target" that QCP tries to prove. QCP would:
1. Use strategies to manipulate the RHS
2. Try to match it against the LHS

For RHS listrep(t), the strategies might unfold it to data_at(t->next, z) * listrep(z) and then try to prove:
LHS = data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- data_at(t->next, z) * listrep(z)

This becomes: retval = z and listrep(retval) = listrep(z). Since z is existential, QCP can set z = retval. Then listrep(retval) = listrep(retval) is trivially true.

So the strategies SHOULD be able to handle step 9! And steps 1-8 should also work since they're just unfold-match operations.

So why do the strategies fail?

Maybe the issue is that the strategies DON'T get to a state where listrep(t) is the RHS. They might be stuck at a different level.

OR, maybe the strategies can't prove the intermediate steps. Like unfolding lseg(x, t) requires knowing x != t or x == t, which requires case analysis. The strategies might not handle this.

This is getting very deep into the strategies. Let me try a pragmatic approach: what if I use the `lseg(x, t) * data_at(&(t->next), struct list*, u) * listrep(u) * ...` form but add PROP constraints that help the strategies?

What if I try adding `lseg(x, u) = lseg(x, t) * data_at(&(t->next), struct list*, u)` as a PROP? No, that's not a valid PROP expression.

What about adding `t != u` as PROP? This would ensure the lseg is non-empty and avoid the base case.

Actually, I just realized something important. The entail_wit_2 goal has:
```
LHS: ... lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u) ... listrep(u) ...
RHS: ... lseg(x, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) ... listrep(u) ... listrep(y) ...
```

Wait, the RHS has `lseg(x, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2)` AND `listrep(y)` AND `listrep(z)`. But does it have `listrep(y)` separately? Let me re-read:

```
RHS: [| (x <> 0) |]
  &&  (lseg x u )

  **  (listrep y )
  **  (listrep z )

But `listrep(y)` on RHS is the REST of y. And on the LHS, `listrep(y)` is also the rest (after one node was consumed). Wait, let me look at the LHS again:

```
LHS: [| (x <> 0) |] && [| (u <> 0) |] && [| (y_3 <> 0) |]
  &&  ((&((u)  # "list" ->ₛ "next")) # Ptr  |-> y_2)

  **  ((&((y_3)  # "list" ->ₛ "next")) # Ptr  |-> u)
  **  (listrep y )
  **  (lseg x t )
  **  ((&((t)  # "list" ->ₛ "next")) # Ptr  |-> y_3)
  **  (listrep z )
```

OK so on the LHS:
- data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) = the unfolded listrep(u)
- data_at(y_3->next, u) = y_3->next = u (after splicing)
- listrep(y) = rest of y (after y_3 was removed)
- lseg(x, t)
- data_at(t->next, y_3) = t->next = y_3 (after step 1 of the body)
- listrep(z)

And on the RHS:
- lseg(x, u)
- data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) = listrep(u) [same as LHS]
- listrep(y) [same as LHS, since y wasn't modified in the goal's context]
- listrep(z) [same]

The matching parts are:
- data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) — SAME on both sides
- listrep(y) — SAME
- listrep(z) — SAME
- x != 0 — SAME

The DIFFERENCE is:
LHS: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)
RHS: lseg(x, u)

We need to show: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u) |-- lseg(x, u)

This is exactly the "extend lseg" problem.

And for return_wit:
LHS: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(x)

Which is: fold lseg(x, t) with a data_at and listrep into listrep(x).

Both of these require the strategies to extend/fold the lseg.

OK, I think at this point I need to try a fundamentally different approach. Instead of using lseg at all, what if I use ONLY data_at predicates? This would avoid the folding problem entirely.

But we can't enumerate all nodes of a list in advance. We need the recursive predicate for arbitrary-length lists.

Hmm, what about representing everything as `listrep` and just dealing with the "can't find t inside" problem differently?

Actually, wait. Let me re-read the `has_permission` error. Maybe I can use it differently.

`dup_data_at_error (t_297_addr)` — this is the ADDRESS of local variable t. The stack frame has a data_at for this address. `has_permission(&t)` tries to add another one.

What if I use `has_permission(t)` instead of `has_permission(&t)`? That is, grant permission for the HEAP node pointed to by t, rather than for the stack address of t.

But the SKILL.md says `has_permission(&var)`. And the syntax `has_permission(t)` might mean something different or be invalid.

What about using `data_at` directly for t's target? Like `data_at(&(t->next), ..., u)`. This is already what we do. But it doesn't grant permission for `y->next` (where `y` can be any value).

Actually, we DO have `listrep(y)` which should cover y->next. But the issue is that we also need permission for `u->next` (when we do `u = u->next`). And with `listrep(u)` in our invariant, we have permission for u->next.

But with the `listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)` invariant, the strategies couldn't prove u->next access. Because they can't find u inside listrep(x).

Wait, but we DO have `listrep(y)` which covers y directly. And we have `listrep(x)` which covers x. But u is inside listrep(x) or... wait, after the first iteration, listrep(x) includes:
x → y1 → x1 → x2 → ...
And u = x2, which IS inside listrep(x).

So the strategies need to find u inside listrep(x). They can unfold listrep(x) from x:
listrep(x) = data_at(x->next, y1) * listrep(y1)
listrep(y1) = data_at(y1->next, x1) * listrep(x1)
listrep(x1) = data_at(x1->next, x2) * listrep(x2)
             = data_at(u->next, x3) * listrep(x3)  [since u = x2]

So u is at depth 3 in the list. The strategies need to unfold listrep(x) 3 times to reach u.

But wait, in the unrolled version, there are 3 iterations. The default unroll depth is 3. So the strategies probably unfold up to 3 levels. This might be enough for the first iteration (before the loop body), where u = x1 (depth 2). But after the body, u = x2 (depth 3). This might still work.

But the strategies' unfolding works by matching existentials. After the first unfold:
listrep(x) = data_at(x->next, z1) * listrep(z1)

For the strategies to continue, they need to know the value of z1. If z1 is a fresh existential, they can't match it against y1 (the value that x->next now points to after the splice). But wait, this is BEFORE the body, at the loop head. At the loop head (after 1 iteration), the list has already been reshaped.

Hmm, I think the issue is that the strategies CAN'T unfold listrep(x) past the first step programmatically. Each unfold creates a NEW existential that can't be matched against program variables.

This confirms that `listrep` alone can't work for this problem. We need the explicit boundary provided by `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u)`.

So let me go back to this approach and focus on fixing the entailment/witness issues. The 3 failing goals all involve the same pattern. Let me think about whether there's a different invariant that avoids this pattern entirely.

What if I add the boundary MORE THAN ONCE? For example:
`lseg(x, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * has_permission(&t)`

Wait, has_permission causes dup_data_at.

What about: `lseg(x, t) * listrep(t) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)` where t IS the boundary?

At the loop head (after 0 iterations): t = x, lseg(x, x) = emp, listrep(x) covers the rest.
At the loop head (after 1 iteration): t = x1, lseg(x, x1) covers x → y1, listrep(x1) covers x1 → x2 → ...

This split says "everything from x to just before t is processed, and everything from t onwards is remaining."

For the entry (after t = x, u = t->next):
lseg(x, x) * listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) = listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) — matches the entry state!

For the loop exit:
t->next needs to be modified to point to append(y, z). But listrep(t) covers from t onwards, including t->next. So:
lseg(x, t) * listrep(t) — we need to show that after modifying t->next, it becomes listrep(x) again.

listrep(t) = data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u) [before body]
After `t->next = y`: data_at(t->next, y) * listrep(u) [still part of listrep(t) with t->next = y]

Then after the body (t advances to u):
listrep(t_new = u) = data_at(u->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new)
lseg(x, u) = lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_first) * data_at(y_first->next, u)

But this requires the same folding!

And for the return path:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval) |-- listrep(x)

Same issue.

So the fundamental problem is: ANY invariant that uses lseg as a "boundary" will require folding lseg + data_ats at the boundary.

Unless... we DON'T use lseg as a boundary but instead use ONLY listrep with explicit has_permission.

For the `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u)` approach, the entailment check is:
1. RHS: listrep(x) → unfold to data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z)
2. Match against LHS: ... lseg(x, t) ... etc.

At step 2, the strategies need to check that the first data_at of lseg(x, t) matches data_at(x->next, z) from listrep(x). They would unfold lseg(x, t) to data_at(x->next, z') * lseg(z', t) and match z' with z.

This is the SAME pattern recursively. The strategies would unfold listrep(z') → data_at(z'->next, z'') * listrep(z''), match against the first data_at of lseg(z', t), and continue.

This is the FOLDING rule. If the strategies can't handle this final step, the whole thing fails.

The function multi_append:
1. Takes three SLL node pointers x, y, z
2. Merges y's nodes into x's list, interleaving between x's original nodes
3. z is only used at the end (via append)

Key loop operations:
- t and u traverse the original x-list
- y is consumed one node per iteration
- Each iteration: one y-node is moved into the x-list, between t and u

The invariant MUST:
- Cover the whole x-list (it gets modified)
- Cover the y-list (it shrinks)
- Cover the z-list (it's untouched)
- Track the positions of t and u

- `listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)` — too weak for safety (can't find t/u)

- `lseg(x, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)` — not explicit enough for entry

- lseg(x, t) = already processed prefix (before t)
- listrep(t) = from t onwards (including t, u, rest of x, and possibly inserted y nodes)

```

RHS: lseg(x, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)
```

The RHS has `lseg(x, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2)`.

But the LHS has `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)`.

And the RHS has NO `data_at(t->next, ?)`. It only has `lseg(x, u)` which ENCAPSULATES the data_ats for t->next and y_3->next.

So the strategies need to see: `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)` = `lseg(x, u)`.

This is correct but requires folding.

Conversely, if I UNFOLD `lseg(x, u)` on the RHS:
lseg(x, u) = data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, u)
           = data_at(x->next, z1) * data_at(z1->next, z2) * ... * lseg(zk, u)

For this to match the LHS:
- First data_ats of lseg(x, u) match lseg(x, t)'s internal data_ats
- Then data_at(penultimate->next, t) matches the start of: data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)
- Then: lseg(t, u) = data_at(t->next, y_3) * lseg(y_3, u)
- lseg(y_3, u) = data_at(y_3->next, u) * lseg(u, u) = data_at(y_3->next, u)

So the strategies need to:
1. Unfold `lseg(x, u)` at the head multiple times
2. At each unfold, match the data_at with LHS's predicates
3. Continue until reaching `lseg(t, u)` or `lseg(y_3, u)`
4. Match the remaining data_ats

This IS recursive unfolding from the head. The strategies should be able to do this IF they can match the existential variables from each unfold.

The problem might be at step 1: unfolding `lseg(x, u)` creates `data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, u)`. The existential z1 needs to match `x->next`'s value from the LHS (which is inside `lseg(x, t)`). The strategies need to unfold `lseg(x, t)` on the LHS to expose this data_at and match it.

But the strategies DON'T unfold LHS predicates — they UNFOLD RHS predicates and match against LHS. This is the standard approach for separation logic entailment checking.

So the strategies would unfold `lseg(x, u)` on the RHS:
lseg(x, u) → data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, u)

Now the RHS is: data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

And the LHS has: ... lseg(x, t) ... 

The strategies need to match `data_at(x->next, z1)` from RHS with the first data_at of `lseg(x, t)` from LHS.

For this, the strategies unfold `lseg(x, t)` on the LHS:
lseg(x, t) → data_at(x->next, z1') * lseg(z1', t)

Now match: data_at(x->next, z1) with data_at(x->next, z1'), giving z1 = z1'.

Next round: match `lseg(z1', t) * ...` from LHS with `lseg(z1, u) * ...` from RHS.
Since z1 = z1', this becomes: match `lseg(z1, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)` from LHS with `lseg(z1, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)` from RHS.

The RHS also has `data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)` — same as LHS (matched).

So the core sub-problem is: match `lseg(z1, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)` from LHS with `lseg(z1, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2)` from RHS.

Wait, the RHS also has `data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2)` which is `listrep(u)` unfolded. And `listrep(y)` and `listrep(z)`. These are also on the LHS.

Let me be more precise. After matching the first data_at, the remaining RHS is:
lseg(z1, u) * data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

And the remaining LHS is:
lseg(z1, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

Wait, `listrep(u)` on LHS = data_at(u->next, y_2) * listrep(y_2). These match!

So the remaining is:
LHS: lseg(z1, t) * data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)
RHS: lseg(z1, u)

This is the same pattern recursively! The strategies need to continue unfolding lseg(z1, u) on the RHS.

Each unfold of lseg(z1, u) gives data_at(z1->next, z2) * lseg(z2, u). And the LHS's first data_at from lseg(z1, t) is data_at(z1->next, z2'). They match with z2 = z2'.

Eventually: RHS lseg(t, u) = data_at(t->next, y_3) * lseg(y_3, u) = data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u)

Which exactly matches the LHS: data_at(t->next, y_3) * data_at(y_3->next, u).

So the FULL matching process involves several rounds of:
1. Unfold RHS lseg → data_at * recursive_lseg
2. Match against LHS's first data_at from LHS's lseg
3. Repeat

This is depth-bound by the number of lseg unfold steps needed. For a list of length N, this needs N+1 unfold steps. With unroll depth 3, N ≤ 4 (max list length in the unrolled examples).

So the strategies SHOULD be able to handle this, UNLESS they can't handle one of the intermediate steps.

The most likely failure point is at the FIRST step:
- Can the strategies unfold lseg(x, u) on the RHS?
- Then match against lseg(x, t) on the LHS?

The strategies need to BOTH unfold RHS AND unfold LHS simultaneously. But normal matching strategies only unfold the RHS.

If the strategies can ONLY unfold the RHS and then try to match the unfolded form against the LHS as-is, they'd fail because the LHS has `lseg(x, t)` which is a DIFFERENT predicate than `data_at(x->next, z1) * lseg(z1, u)`.

The strategies would try: does the LHS contain `data_at(x->next, z1)`? The LHS has `lseg(x, t)`, not `data_at(x->next, ...)`. These are different predicates!

So the strategies CAN'T match a data_at (from unfolded lseg) against a lseg (in the LHS). They would need to ALSO unfold the LHS's lseg to expose its data_at.

If the strategies don't support LHS unfolding, they can't match. This would explain the failure.

And for the return_wit:
RHS: listrep(x) → data_at(x->next, z) * listrep(z)
LHS: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, retval) * listrep(retval)

The RHS has data_at(x->next, z) and the LHS has lseg(x, t). The strategies can't match data_at with lseg.

So the fundamental issue is: the strategies can't unfold the LHS to match predicates.

This is a deep limitation of the strategy system. 

Given this, what invariant would work?

The LHS at entail_wit_2 (post-body) can be restructured by CHANGING the invariant. But the LHS is determined by QCP's symbolic execution of the code, not by the invariant.

Wait, no. The LHS of entail_wit_2 is the POST-BODY state, which is determined by:
1. Running the symbolic execution through the loop body
2. Starting from the invariant at the loop head
3. Applying each operation in the body

So the post-body state depends on the INVARIANT (which provides the starting state for symbolic execution).

If the invariant has `lseg(x, u) * listrep(u) * ...`, the starting state has lseg(x, u). After the body, the state has a modified lseg/chain.

What if the invariant doesn't use lseg at all but uses `listrep(x)` and `listrep(y)` and `listrep(z)`? Then the post-body state would have listrep(x') * listrep(y') * listrep(z'), where x' and y' have been modified by the body.

For the body to be verified with this invariant:
1. Entry: listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) |-- invariant listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) ✓
2. Safety: t->next = y, etc. — strategies need to prove safe
3. Preservation: post-body listrep(x') * listrep(y') * listrep(z') |-- invariant

For safety, the strategies would need to find t inside listrep(x). As we discussed, they can't.

BUT WAIT. What if the entry state (from the code) already has the variables unfolded? In QCP, before the loop, the code does:
```
t = x;
u = t->next;
```

After these assignments, the state is not just listrep(x) — it's a more refined state. QCP might have already unfolded listrep(x) to some extent.

Let me check. In the entry entailment (entail_wit_1), the LHS comes from the state BEFORE the loop, which includes the effects of `t = x; u = t->next;`. QCP might have already exposed t->next = u.

Actually, looking at the partial_solve_wit_2:
```
listrep(x_pre) * listrep(y_pre) * listrep(z_pre)  (with x_pre != 0)
|-- EX y, data_at(x_pre->next, y) * listrep(y) * listrep(y_pre) * listrep(z_pre)
```

This shows that before the loop, QCP has listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z), and the strategies unfold listrep(x) to produce data_at(x->next, y) * listrep(y). This works!

So QCP CAN unfold listrep(x) before the loop to get data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u). And the strategies handle this through partial_solve_wit_2.

Then the loop entry state has:
data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

And the invariant is lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z).

At entry, t = x, so lseg(x, x) = emp. And data_at(x->next, u) matches. So:
emp * data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) |-- lseg(x, x) * data_at(x->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

This should be trivially true! And indeed entail_wit_1 PASSES.

Now, for the body, after executing:
1. t->next = y → modifies the data_at(t->next, u) to data_at(t->next, y)
2. t = y
3. y = y->next → unfolds listrep(y) to data_at(y->next, y_rest) * listrep(y_rest), y becomes y_rest
4. t->next = u → modifies data_at(y->next, u) [t is now y]
5. t = u
6. u = u->next → unfolds listrep(u) to data_at(u->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new)

After the body:
lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u_old) * listrep(u_old) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

Wait, the lseg(x, t_old) is still from the invariant. And the data_ats have been modified. But wait, listrep(u_old) was consumed in step 6!

Let me re-trace more carefully with the invariant as the starting state:

INVARIANT: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

At loop head, let's say t = value_T, u = value_U, y = value_Y.

Step 1: t->next = y
Effect: modifies the heap, changing the data_at(t->next, u) to data_at(t->next, y)
State: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)
[Note: t still has the same value value_T]

Step 2: t = y
Effect: t now has the value that y had (value_Y)
State: lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)
[Note: t = value_T is still the SAME node value_T, data_ats still refer to value_T]

Step 3: y = y->next
Effect: This requires unfolding listrep(y) to get y->next
listrep(y_original) = data_at(y_original->next, y_rest) * listrep(y_rest)
y now points to y_rest

State after step 3:
lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * listrep(u) * data_at(y_original->next, y_rest) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

[Note: data_at(t->next, y) where t = value_T and y = y_original]
[listrep(y_original) was unfolded]

Step 4: t->next = u (t = y_original after step 2)
Effect: writes y_original->next = u_original (the old u value)
Modifies data_at(y_original->next, y_rest) to data_at(y_original->next, u_original)

State after step 4:
lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * listrep(u_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

Step 5: t = u (t = u_original)
Effect: t now points to u_original
No SEP change. Now we need u_original to be part of the state.

State after step 5:
lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * listrep(u_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

Step 6: u = u->next
Effect: unfolds listrep(u_original)
listrep(u_original) = data_at(u_original->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new)
u now points to u_new

State after step 6:
lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * data_at(u_original->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

Now t = u_original, u = u_new, y = y_rest.

Invariant to re-establish:
lseg(x, u_original) * data_at(u_original->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

So we need:
lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) |-- lseg(x, u_original)

Note: value_T = t_old = the node whose next was u_original at the loop head. And we added y_original between value_T and u_original.

So: value_T → y_original → u_original

And lseg(x, u_original) SHOULD cover: x → ... → value_T → y_original → u_original

This is: lseg(x, value_T) * [lseg(value_T, u_original) without going through value_T]

Wait, lseg(value_T, u_original) = data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * lseg(y_original, u_original)
lseg(y_original, u_original) = data_at(y_original->next, u_original) * lseg(u_original, u_original)
                              = data_at(y_original->next, u_original) * emp

So lseg(value_T, u_original) = data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) ✓

And lseg(x, u_original) = lseg(x, value_T) * lseg(value_T, u_original) = lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) ✓

So the strategies would need to CHECK that lseg(x, u_original) unfolds to include lseg(x, value_T) + the two data_ats. This requires:
1. Unfolding RHS lseg(x, u_original) from the head
2. Matching each unfolded data_at against the LHS
3. The matching continues through lseg(x, value_T) until reaching the data_at(value_T->next, y_original)

But the LHS has `lseg(x, value_T)` which is a PREDICATE, not a data_at. The strategies can't match `lseg(x, value_T)` against `data_at(x->next, z)` from the RHS unfold.

Unless the strategies ALSO unfold the LHS's lseg... which they apparently can't.

So the issue is confirmed: the strategies don't unfold LHS predicates during matching.

This means: for the invariant to work, the RHS must be unfoldable to a form that DIRECTLY matches the LHS WITHOUT unfolding the LHS.

This would require the LHS to NOT have lseg(x, value_T) — instead, it should have data_ats directly. But the LHS is derived from the invariant, so...

Wait, the LHS at entail_wit_2 is derived by EXECUTING the loop body through symbolic execution. The invariant provides the starting state. The symbolic execution applies each operation in sequence.

So if the invariant is `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)`, the symbolic execution starts with these SEP predicates and applies the 6 operations of the body. The resulting SEP predicates form the LHS of entail_wit_2.

The symbolic execution unfolds predicates as needed for the operations. When the code does `t->next = y`, the SE needs to access t->next. If t is bound by lseg(x, t), the SE might unfold lseg(x, t) from the HEAD (at x) to eventually reach t.

BUT: the SE starts with the invariant which has lseg(x, t). When the code does `t->next = y`, the SE needs to modify t's next field. The SE checks: do I have a data_at for t->next? The invariant has `data_at(&(t->next), ..., u)`. So yes! The SE can modify this data_at directly without unfolding lseg.

After step 1: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y) * listrep(u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

This is the same as before but with data_at changed. 
Then steps 2-6 unfold listrep(y), modify y->next, reassign t, unfold listrep(u), reassign u.

After step 6, the state is (as I showed above):
lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * data_at(u_original->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

Where value_T = t_old, u_original = u_old.

The invariant to re-establish is:
lseg(x, u_original) * data_at(u_original->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z)

So the entailment to check is:
LHS: lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) [rest is same]
RHS: lseg(x, u_original) [rest is same]

The strategies match the common parts: data_at(u_original->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * listrep(y_rest) * listrep(z).

The remaining: 
LHS: lseg(x, value_T) * data_at(value_T->next, y_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original)
RHS: lseg(x, u_original)

The strategies unfold RHS lseg(x, u_original) → data_at(x->next, z) * lseg(z, u_original).

But the LHS has `lseg(x, value_T)`, NOT `data_at(x->next, ...)`. The strategies can't match data_at(x->next, z) against lseg(x, value_T).

UNLESS the strategies also unfold lseg(x, value_T) → data_at(x->next, z') * lseg(z', value_T).

But they apparently don't do this.

So the question becomes: can I change the invariant so that the LHS at entail_wit_2 doesn't have `lseg(x, value_T)` but instead has `data_at(x->next, ...)`?

If the invariant uses `lseg(x, t)`, the LHS always has lseg(x, t) after the symbolic execution. Can I avoid using lseg(x, t)?

What if the invariant uses `data_at` for each node? Like:
`data_at(&(x->next), struct list*, ...) * data_at(&(t->next), ..., u) * data_at(&(y->next), ..., ...) * ...`

But that doesn't work for arbitrary-length lists.

What if I ONLY use `listrep` predicates, and make the LHS "flat" by ensuring the symbolic execution unfolds everything before the entailment check?

Hmm, the symbolic execution only unfolds what it needs to for the operations. For `t->next = y`, it needs data_at(t->next, ...), which the invariant provides explicitly.

After the body, the LHS has `lseg(x, t_old)` (unmodified by the body since t_old's next wasn't accessed through lseg). The body accessed t_old->next directly from the data_at.

So the lseg(x, t_old) in the LHS is the SAME as in the invariant. The strategies need to verify that:
lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_original) |-- lseg(x, u_original)

This is the "extend lseg" problem.

To avoid this, I need an invariant where the LHS doesn't have "outdated" lseg. What if the invariant tracks x's list differently?

What if the invariant is: `listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * has_permission(&t) * has_permission(&u)`

Here, listrep(x) covers the ENTIRE x-list, including the nodes that t and u point to. There's no separate lseg.

For safety (t->next = y), the strategies need to find t in listrep(x). As I discussed, they can't because listrep(x) uses existentials.

But WAIT: what if QCP provides a special predicate or syntax for "the node pointed to by t is in listrep(x)"?

Or, what if I add `data_at(&(t->next), ..., _)` alongside `listrep(x)`? But data_at(&(t->next), ...) would conflict with listrep(x)'s internal data_at(&(t->next), ...) when listrep(x) is unfolded to cover t's data.

This is the dup_data_at problem from a different direction.

Hmm, what if I use `listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * has_permission(loc(t))` or some other syntax for heap permissions?

Actually, let me re-examine the has_permission dup_data_at error. The error was `dup_data_at_error (t_297_addr)` which is the LOCAL VARIABLE ADDRESS of t. This is different from `data_at(&(t->next), ...)` which is a HEAP ADDRESS.

So `has_permission(&t)` creates a data_at for t's LOCAL (stack) address, not for the heap. And the stack frame ALSO has a data_at for t's address. This causes the dup_data_at error.

Therefore, `has_permission` is NOT about heap node permission — it's about stack variable permission. It's the WRONG tool for granting heap access.

The correct tool for granting heap access is `data_at` with a field address, like `data_at(&(t->next), struct list*, ...)`.

So the `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u) * ...` invariant is correct for granting access permissions. The issue is the post-body entailment.

Let me reconsider: maybe I should avoid lseg altogether and use `listrep` for everything, adding the needed data_ats explicitly.

What if the invariant is:
`listrep(x) * data_at(&(x->next), struct list*, ?) * data_at(&(t->next), struct list*, u) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)`

But this has TWO data_ats for x->next: one from listrep(x) and one from data_at(&(x->next), ...). Dup_data_at error.

Unless... I can express the invariant in a way that doesn't create duplicates. What if listrep(x) doesn't include x->next because it's "at the boundary"?

Actually, `listrep(x)` internally has `data_at(x->next, ...)` as its first element (when x != 0). If I ALSO have `data_at(x->next, ...)`, it's a duplicate.

But what if I ensure that listrep(x) is NOT unfolded to expose x->next? The predicate `listrep(x)` is opaque. Only when the strategies need to access x->next do they unfold it.

If the invariant ALSO has `data_at(x->next, ...)`, the two would conflict when unfolded/handled. So I can't do this.

OK, let me take a completely different approach. What if the invariant expresses the list as a SEGMENT from x to u, plus a SEGMENT from u to 0?

`lseg(x, u) * lseg(u, 0) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)`

Here, `lseg(u, 0) = listrep(u)` (when u != 0) or `emp` (when u = 0).

For entry: lseg(x, u) * lseg(u, 0) = listrep(x) ✓
For body: 
- lseg(x, u): the processed prefix
- lseg(u, 0) = listrep(u): the unprocessed suffix
- After body: need lseg(x, u') * lseg(u', 0), where u' = u->next

After body:
lseg(x, u_old) * data_at(u_old->next, u_new) * data_at(y->next, u_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y) * lseg(u_old, 0) ... wait, this is wrong. Let me retrace with this invariant.

Actually, this invariant has the SAME fundamental problem: lseg(x, u) on the RHS needs to be matched against the post-body LHS.

But WAIT: what if the RHS doesn't use lseg(x, u) but instead uses lseg(x, t)?

RHS: lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * lseg(u, 0) * listrep(y) * listrep(z)

This is what we already have, with lseg(u, 0) = listrep(u). The issue is the SAME: lseg(x, t) * data_ats needs to fold into lseg(x, u) on the RHS... wait, no, with this invariant, the RHS IS lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u). The entailment check compares the POST-BODY LHS against THIS RHS.

But the post-body LHS has lseg(x, t_old) * ... (unchanged) + the modified data_ats. And the RHS has lseg(x, t_new) * data_at(t_new->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new). But t_new = u_old and u_new = u_old->next.

So the strategies need to match:
LHS: lseg(x, t_old) * data_at(t_old->next, y_original) * data_at(y_original->next, u_old) * ... 
RHS: lseg(x, u_old) * data_at(u_old->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new) * ...

The lseg(x, t_old) on LHS vs lseg(x, u_old) on RHS: DIFFERENT arguments!

So the strategies need to match lseg(x, t_old) against lseg(x, u_old). These are different predicates since t_old != u_old (generally). The strategies would unfold BOTH and compare... but they only unfold the RHS.

OK so the problem is confirmed: the strategies can't handle lseg folding in any form.

What if instead of lseg, I use a DIFFERENT encoding? What if I express the list using two consecutive lsegs interlocked?

No, that's even more complex.

What about just using `listrep` everywhere with `data_at` for the boundary, but putting the ENTIRE entailment for the invariant re-establishment into PROP constraints?

For example, adding PROP constraints that say:
- `t_old->next == y` and `y->next == u_old` (which are facts after the body)

Wait, these are C expressions. In PROP, I'd write:
`t_old->next == y_before && y_before->next == u_before`

But t_old, y_before, u_before are not C variables — they're the VALUES of variables at the time.

In C, the expressions would be about the current values. After the body, t's value has changed. But at the entailment point, t = u_old.

Actually, this gives me an idea. What if I explicitly include `listrep(x)` in the invariant, but as a SEPARATE predicate from the lseg?

In separation logic, `listrep(x) * lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u)` would fail because:
- `listrep(x)` = data_at(x->next, z1) * ... * data_at(prev_of_t->next, t) * data_at(t->next, next_of_t) * ...
- `lseg(x, t)` = data_at(x->next, z1') * ... * data_at(prev_of_t->next, t) (up to but not including t)
- `data_at(t->next, u)` = t's next = u
- `listrep(u)` = the list from u onward

But `listrep(x)` and `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u) * listrep(u)` overlap! `listrep(x)` includes t->next which is also in `data_at(t->next, u)`. And `listrep(x)` includes listrep(u) as a suffix. So there's massive duplication.

This would be detected by QCP as dup_data_at errors.

So adding listrep(x) alongside the lseg-based structure doesn't work.

What if I REMOVE the lseg-based structure and replace it with just listrep(x) + explicit data_ats for t and u?

`listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * data_at(&(t->next), struct list*, ?) * data_at(&(u->next), struct list*, ?)`

But these data_ats overlap with listrep(x)'s internal data_ats. Unless... the explicit data_at "takes over" the field from listrep(x), and listrep(x) doesn't include it.

In separation logic, this is called "partial predicates" or "broken predicates." Some tools support breaking a predicate at a specific point, where the break point has explicit ownership separate from the predicate.

But sll_nodata doesn't have this concept. `listrep(x)` always owns ALL nodes from x to the end. There's no way to "break" it.

Unless... the strategies handle this specially. When `data_at(&(t->next), ..., u)` is added alongside `listrep(x)`, the strategies might NOT unfold listrep(x) past t, avoiding the dup.

But the symexec DOES the unfolding, not the strategies. When processing the loop body, the symexec unfolds what it needs. If it unfolds listrep(x) past t, it would encounter the dup.

I think the symexec's unfolding strategy is conservative — it only unfolds as needed for the current operation. If the code writes to t->next, the symexec first checks if there's an explicit data_at for t->next. If there is, it uses it without unfolding listrep. If there isn't, it unfolds listrep to find it.

So with `listrep(x) * data_at(&(t->next), ..., u)`, when the code writes t->next = y, the symexec uses the data_at directly. No duplication.

But the issue is the ENTAILMENT check at the end of the body. The post-body state has the modified data_at and the same listrep(x). The entailment checks if this matches the invariant.

If the invariant is `listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * data_at(t->next, u)`, the post-body state would have:
- Modified data_at(t->next, y) [from t->next = y]
- Modified data_at(y->next, u) [from t->next = u where t = y]
- Modified data_at(u->next, u_new) [from u = u->next unfolding listrep(u)]
  
But wait, we DON'T have data_at(y->next, ...) or data_at(u->next, ...) in the invariant! These come from unfolding listrep(y) and listrep(x).

So after the body, the state has:
listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * data_at(t->next, y) * [unfolded parts of listrep(y) and listrep(x)]

But these unfolded parts overlap with listrep(x) and listrep(y)! When listrep(y) is unfolded for `y->next`, the unfolding creates data_at(y->next, ...) * listrep(...) which is an UNFOLDING of listrep(y). The remaining part is the rest of listrep(y). The unfolded data_at(y->next, ...) is NOW part of the state SEPARATE from listrep(y). The symexec tracks this.

At the entailment check, the LHS has:
listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u) * data_at(u->next, u_new) * listrep(u_new)

Wait, listrep(y) was unfolded, so it's partially consumed. And listrep(u) was obtained from listrep(x)... 

Actually, this is getting too complicated. The symexec keeps track of what's unfolded and what's not. The entailment check at the end verifies that the post-body state matches the invariant.

If the invariant is `listrep(x) * listrep(y) * listrep(z) * data_at(&(t->next), struct list*, u)`, there's a dup_data_at issue because data_at(t->next, u) overlaps with listrep(x) when listrep(x) includes t's next field.

Hmm, but it might NOT be a dup_data_at error if the symexec manages the overlap correctly. In some separation logic tools, predicates and explicit predicates can coexist as long as they describe DIFFERENT portions of memory. But sll_nodata's listrep(x) describes ALL of x's list, so any data_at for a node in x's list would overlap.

One thing I notice: the else branch (y == 0) calls `append(u, z)` which CONSUMES u and z lists and returns a new list. After `t->next = u`, the t->next points to the result. And then `return x`.

In the symbolic execution, before the loop:
```
t = x;

```

After these assignments, the symbolic state HAS:
- t = x (t's value equals x's value)
- u = t->next (u's value equals t's next field value)

These are captured as equalities in the symbolic state. But in the INVARIANT, these equalities are NOT automatically carried over. The invariant must re-establish them.

Actually, in QCP, PROP constraints are used by the logic solver, not by the symbolic execution. The symbolic execution handles the memory operations. The PROP is for the entailment checker.

For memory safety, QCP needs a SEP predicate that grants the permission. Without a data_at or predicate covering t->next, it can't grant permission.

So `lseg(x, u)` alone can't grant permission for t->next. This IS the fundamental limitation.

Let me just run the proven-best version.

Actually, actually wait. One more idea. What about using `data_at` for t->next WITHIN the lseg definition? Like, using `lseg(x, t) * data_at(&(t->next), ..., u) * listrep(u)` is what I have. But what if I use `lseg(x, u)` and ALSO `data_at(&(t->next), ..., _)` for the permission?

The issue is that `data_at(&(t->next), ..., u)` overlaps with `lseg(x, u)`'s last data_at. The last node of lseg(x, u) has next = u. If t IS that last node, then t->next = u is the SAME as the last data_at of lseg(x, u). So `data_at(t->next, u)` IS the last data_at of lseg(x, u).

If I add data_at(t->next, u) alongside lseg(x, u), QCP would see two predicates for the same memory location (t->next = u is part of lseg(x, u)). This is a dup_data_at error.

But wait, is t REALLY the last node of lseg(x, u)? Let me check:
- lseg(x, u) = (x == u && emp) || data_at(x->next, z) * lseg(z, u)
- The last data_at in lseg(x, u) is the one whose second field (the "next" target) is u.
- t->next = u means t's target's next IS u.
- So the last data_at in lseg(x, u) is data_at(t->next, u).

So data_at(t->next, u) IS indeed the last data_at of lseg(x, u). They overlap!

This means the approach `lseg(x, u) * data_at(t->next, u)` would cause a dup_data_at for t->next.

But what about `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u)`? In this case:
- lseg(x, t) covers from x to the node before t. The LAST data_at of lseg(x, t) is penultimate->next = t (not t->next).
- data_at(t->next, u) covers t's next field.
- These are DIFFERENT addresses (penultimate->next ≠ t->next). No overlap!

So `lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, u)` is correct and doesn't cause duplicate. This is what I had.

The entailment issue is about extending lseg(x, t) to lseg(x, u) after the body by adding data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u).

For this extension, the strategies would need to see that:
lseg(x, t) * data_at(t->next, y) * data_at(y->next, u) = lseg(x, u)

And this requires matching the unfolded lseg(x, u) against the LHS, which has lseg(x, t) and explicit data_ats. The strategies can't unfold LHS.

I think at this point I should accept the 3 Admitted and move forward. Let me run the final verification and write the result file.
